Jointly Modeling and Clustering Tensors in High Dimensions
Biao Cai, Jingfei Zhang, Will Wei Sun

Abstract
We consider the problem of jointly modeling and clustering populations of tensors by introducing a high-dimensional tensor mixture model with heterogeneous covariances. To effectively tackle the high dimensionality of tensor objects, we employ plausible dimension reduction assumptions that exploit the intrinsic structures of tensors such as low-rankness in the mean and separability in the covariance. In estimation, we develop an efficient high-dimensional expectation-conditional-maximization ( HECM ) algorithm that breaks the intractable optimization in the M-step into a sequence of much simpler conditional optimization problems, each of which is convex, admits regularization and has closed-form updating formulas. Our theoretical analysis is challenged by both the non-convexity in the EM-type estimation and having access to only the solutions of conditional maximizations in the M-step, leading to the notion of dual non-convexity. We demonstrate that the proposed HECM algorithm, with an appropriate initialization, converges geometrically to a neighborhood that is within statistical precision of the true parameter. The efficacy of our proposed method is demonstrated through comparative numerical experiments and an application to a medical study, where our proposal achieves an improved clustering accuracy over existing benchmarking methods.



Keywords: expectation conditional maximization; computational and statistical errors; tensor clustering; tensor decomposition; unsupervised learning.

1
Introduction

In modern data science, tensor data, where the data take the form of a multidimensional array, are becoming ubiquitous in a wide variety of scientific and business applications.
For example, in recommender systems, the data are collected as a three-way (user, item, context) tensor
(
Bi et al. 2018
)
, where the context can be item features such as time, location and publisher. Due to the rapidly increasing interest in analyzing tensor data, the literature on tensor data analysis is fast growing, including topics such as tensor decomposition
(
Anandkumar et al. 2014a
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
)
, tensor completion
(
Cai et al. 2020
;
Xia et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2021
)
, and tensor regression
(
Li and Zhang 2017
;
Zhang et al. 2018
;
Zhou et al. 2023
)
. We refer to a recent survey by
Bi et al. 2020
for a comprehensive review on tensor data analysis.

In this paper, we consider the problem of jointly modeling and clustering populations of tensors. When tensors are collected from heterogeneous populations, an important task is to cluster the tensor samples into homogeneous groups and characterize distributions of the different populations. This finds applications in face clustering
(
Cao et al. 2014
)
, video summarization
(
Rabbouch et al. 2017
)
, brain imaging segmentation
(
Mirzaei and Adeli 2018
)
, user clickstream clustering
(
Wang et al. 2016
)
, among others. An intrinsic challenge in modeling and clustering tensors is the high dimensionality of tensor objects. For example, in our real data analysis in Section
7
, there are
n=57
tensor objects to be modeled and clustered, each of dimension
116\times 116\times 30
yielding
403,680
entries. To perform clustering, one may first vectorize the tensor objects and then apply clustering techniques developed for high-dimensional vectors
(
Wang et al. 2015
;
Hao et al. 2017
;
Cai et al. 2019
)
. However, as the structures in tensors are largely ignored after vectorization, these vector-based approaches can result in a loss of information, leading to reduced efficiency and accuracy. Another approach is to consider tensor subspace clustering methods, which find latent cluster structures embedded in one or more modes of a
single
tensor
(
Sun and Li 2019
;
Chi et al. 2020
)
. When
n
tensor samples are available, it seems sensible to stack them into one higher-order tensor, where the last mode is of dimension
n
, and then apply a tensor subspace clustering method to recover cluster labels along the last mode. However, this approach has one fundamental limitation as clustering along one mode of a
single
tensor inevitably runs into the curse of dimensionality, in that the clustering accuracy is expected to deteriorate with
n
, the dimension of the last mode. As shown in
Sun and Li 2019
;
Chi et al. 2020
, to consistently estimate labels along one mode of a tensor, the dimension of this mode must be small compared to others. This condition seems unnatural under our setting as the clustering accuracy is expected to improve with the sample size
n
.

Recently, some progress has been made for clustering a collection of tensors. Specifically,
Tait and McNicholas 2019
considered a mixture model estimated using a standard EM algorithm. Without any dimension reduction assumption on the tensor mean, this method could not handle cases where the sample size is smaller than the total number of tensor entries.
Mai et al. 2021
proposed
DEEM
, which clusters tensors using a carefully designed discriminant analysis and the discriminant tensors are assumed to be sparse. The main focus of
DEEM
was to develop a clustering rule while subpopulation distributions were not directly estimated. Characterizing subpopulation distributions can be useful, as one might wish to examine the differences in means and covariances across subpopulations. Moreover,
DEEM
assumed homogeneous covariances across clusters and may not perform well when covariances differ among clusters. As shown in the numerical experiments in Sections
6
-
7
,
DEEM
can be numerically unstable and sensitive to potential model misspecifications.

In this paper, we introduce a flexible high-dimensional tensor mixture model with heterogeneous covariances to jointly model and cluster a collection of tensors.
To facilitate estimability and interpretability, we employ effective dimension reduction assumptions that take advantage of the intrinsic structures of tensors and improve model interpretability. Specifically, we assume the tensor means to be low-rank and internally sparse (defined in Section
2.3
), and the tensor covariances to be separable and conditionally sparse. These assumptions are plausible in a wide range of applications and are commonly employed in the tensor analysis literature
(
Anandkumar et al. 2014a
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Pan et al. 2019
;
Mai et al. 2021
;
Zhou et al. 2023
)
. The mixture components in our proposal are allowed to have heterogeneous covariances, which greatly relaxes the homogeneous and/or isotropic covariance assumption commonly employed in the mixture model literature
(
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
;
Cai et al. 2019
;
Mai et al. 2021
)
.

In estimation, we consider a high-dimensional expectation-maximization (EM) type algorithm. One major challenge is that the M-step in the standard EM algorithm
(
Dempster et al. 1977
)
requires an optimization with respect to the low-rank tensor means and separable covariances from each mixture component. This is an intractable non-convex optimization problem. To tackle this challenge, we propose a high-dimensional expectation-conditional-maximization (
HECM
) algorithm that breaks the challenging optimization problem in the M-step into several simpler alternating conditional optimization problems, each of which is convex, has closed-form updating formulas and admits regularization. An attractive property of the proposed
HECM
algorithm is that sparsity structures can be easily incorporated into parameter estimation by adding regularization to the smaller conditional optimizations.

While convergence to an arbitrary fixed point has been studied for ECM-type algorithms
(
Meng 1994
)
, to our knowledge, local convergence has yet to be investigated, even in the low-dimensional regime. In our theoretical analysis, we show that the
HECM
algorithm converges geometrically to a neighborhood that is within statistical precision of the unknown true parameter given a suitable initialization. This is a useful statistical guarantee that sheds light on when and how quickly the
HECM
iterates converge to the true parameter. Our theoretical analysis is highly nontrivial, as the conditional updating scheme in the
HECM
requires a delicate treatment in order to establish the contraction of the iterations. In particular, our analysis builds on a collection of conditional
Q
functions in the form of
Q_{n}(\bm{\vartheta},\bar{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\bm{\vartheta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
, where
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}
is the parameter update from the
t
-th step,
\bm{\vartheta}
is a subset of
{\bm{\Theta}}
to be updated in the
(t+1)
-th step and
\bar{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\bm{\vartheta}}
collects all other parameters being conditioned on, with some taking values from the
t
-th step (i.e., those yet to be updated in
\bar{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\bm{\vartheta}}
) and some from the
(t+1)
-th step (i.e., those already updated in
\bar{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\bm{\vartheta}}
).
As the
HECM
does not have access to
\arg\max_{{\bm{\Theta}}}Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
in the M-step, existing arguments and techniques in the population and sample-based analysis of the standard EM algorithms
(
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
)
are no longer directly applicable. Our analysis is accomplished by identifying new statistical and computational properties of the conditional
Q
functions and employing new proof strategies in establishing the one-step contraction; see Section
4.2
. Although the ECM algorithm has been used in other problems, such as the (vector) t-distribution mixture model
(
Andrews et al. 2011
)
, its local convergence has not been studied before. To our knowledge, our work is the first statistical guarantee on the local convergence of ECM algorithms where the M-step includes in a sequence of conditional maximizations. In addition, we study the convergence rate of the ECM algorithm when the number of clusters is over-specified and when the signal-to-noise ratio diminishes with sample size
n
. Our analyses show that the convergence rate can be much slower in these settings.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the high-dimensional tensor mixture model with heterogeneous covariances. Section
3
discusses the
HECM
algorithm. Section
4
investigates the statistical properties of our proposed method. Section
5
provides theoretical analyses under over-specified mixtures and low signal-to-noise ratio settings. Section
6
presents numerical experiments and Section
7
illustrates with a real data analysis. A discussion section concludes the paper.

2
Model and problem

2.1
Notation and tensor algebra

A
tensor
is a multidimensional array and the
order
of a tensor is the number of dimensions, also referred to as
modes
. We denote vectors using lower-case bold letters (e.g.,
{\bm{x}}
), matrices using upper-case bold letters (e.g.,
{\bm{X}}
), high-order tensors using upper-case bold script letters (e.g.,
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
), and let
[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Given a vector
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we let
\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|_{0}
,
\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|_{1}
and
\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|_{2}
denote the vector
\ell_{0}
,
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
norms, respectively. We use
{\bm{x}}_{j}
or
{\bm{x}}(j)
to denote the
j
-th entry of
{\bm{x}}
.
Given a matrix
{\bm{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, we let
\left\|{\bm{X}}\right\|_{0,\text{off}}=\sum_{i\neq j}\bm{1}({\bm{X}}_{ij}\neq 0)
,
\left\|{\bm{X}}\right\|_{1,\text{off}}=\sum_{i\neq j}|{\bm{X}}_{ij}|
, and
\left\|{\bm{X}}\right\|
denote the off-diagonal
\ell_{0}
,
\ell_{1}
norms and spectral norm, respectively. The vectorization of
{\bm{X}}
is defined as
\mathrm{vec}({\bm{X}})=({\bm{X}}_{11},\ldots,{\bm{X}}_{d_{1}1},\ldots,{\bm{X}}_{1d_{2}},\ldots,{\bm{X}}_{d_{1}d_{2}})^{\top}
.
We use
{\bm{x}}_{i,j}
or
{\bm{x}}(i,j)
to denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
{\bm{X}}
, and
\sigma_{\min}(\cdot)
and
\sigma_{\max}(\cdot)
denote the smallest and largest eigenvalues of a matrix, respectively.
Given a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, its Frobenius norm is defined as
\left\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\right\|_{\text{F}}=\left(\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{M}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}
,
and its max norm is defined as
\left\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\right\|_{\max}=\max_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}\left|\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}\right|
.
For two positive sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, write
a_{n}\precsim b_{n}
or
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if there exist
c>0
and
N>0
such that
a_{n}<cb_{n}
for all
n>N
, and
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
; moreover, write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
a_{n}\precsim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\precsim a_{n}
.

Given a third-order tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
, its mode-1, 2 and 3 fibers are denoted as
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:jk}
,
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i:k}
and
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{ij:}
, respectively. Given a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, the mode-
m
unfolding, denoted as
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}
, arranges the mode-
m
fibers to be the columns of the resulting matrix. For example, the mode-1 unfolding of a third-order tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
can be written as
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{(1)}=[\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:11},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:d_{2}1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:d_{2}d_{3}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times(d_{2}d_{3})}
. The vectorization of tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, denoted as
\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})
, is obtained by stacking the mode-1 fibers of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
. For example, given a third-order tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
, we have
\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})=\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:11}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:d_{2}1}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:d_{2}d_{3}}^{\top}\right)^{\top}
. See Figure
1
for an example of mode-1 fibers, mode-1 unfolding and vectorization.

Figure 1:
Tensor fibers, unfolding and vectorization.

For
\bm{\mathcal{X}},\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, define their inner product as

\left\langle\bm{\mathcal{X}},\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\right\rangle=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}
.
For a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
and a matrix
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{m}}
, the
m
-mode tensor matrix product is denoted as
\times_{m}
and element-wise we have

\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}\times_{m}{\bm{A}}\right)_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m-1},j,i_{m+1},\ldots,i_{M}}=\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{d_{m}}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}{\bm{A}}_{ji_{m}}.

It is easy to see that if
\bm{\mathcal{U}}=\bm{\mathcal{X}}\times_{m}{\bm{A}}
, then
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{(m)}={\bm{A}}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}
.
Given a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
and a list of matrices
{\bm{A}}=\left\{{\bm{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{A}}_{M}\right\}
, where
{\bm{A}}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
,
m\in[M]
, their product
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\times{\bm{A}}
is defined as
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\times{\bm{A}}=\bm{\mathcal{X}}\times_{1}{\bm{A}}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{M}{\bm{A}}_{M}
.
We summarize these notations and algebra in Table
1

and provide an illustrative numerical example in Section
F1
. More discussions of tensor algebra and notations can be found in Section 2 of
Kolda and Bader 2009
.

Notation

Description

(j,k)
-th mode-
1
fiber of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}

\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{:jk}=(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1jk},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{d_{1}jk})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}

i
-th mode-
(2,3)
slice of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}

.

,

matrix Kronecker product

{\bm{A}}\otimes\bm{B}=(a_{ij}\bm{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{pL\times qd_{1}}

Definitions given
\bm{\mathcal{X}},\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
,
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{1}}
,
\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
,
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

2.2
Separable covariance and tensor normal distribution

We start our introduction with third-order tensors. A random tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
is said to have a separable covariance structure if
\text{Cov}\{\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\}={\bm{\Sigma}}_{3}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}
,
where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product. Here,
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
,
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times d_{3}}
represent covariances among the mode-1, mode-2 and mode-3 fibers, respectively. Such a covariance model provides a parsimonious and stable alternative to an otherwise unstructured and unrestricted large covariance matrix
\text{Cov}\{\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\}
of dimension
d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}\times d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}
.

A random tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
is said to follow a tensor normal distribution
(
Lyu et al. 2019
)
with mean
\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
and covariance
\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}=\{{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{\Sigma}}_{M}\}
,
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
, if

{\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}),{\bm{\Sigma}}_{M}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\right).}

When
M=3
and
\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}),{\bm{\Sigma}}_{3}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}_{2}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}\right)
, it holds that
\bm{\mathcal{X}}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}+\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\times_{1}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}_{1}\times_{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}_{2}\times_{3}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}_{3}
,
where
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
is a random tensor with independent standard normal entries.
We denote the tensor normal distribution as
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{T}\left(\bm{\mathcal{U}},\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}\right)
, and the probability density function of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
is

f\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}|\,\bm{\mathcal{U}},\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}\right)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\left\{\prod_{m=1}^{M}|{\bm{\Sigma}}_{m}|^{-d/(2d_{m})}\right\}\exp\left(-\left\|(\bm{\mathcal{X}}-\bm{\mathcal{U}})\times\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{-1/2}\right\|_{F}^{2}/2\right),

(1)

where
d=\prod_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}
and
\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{-1/2}=\{{\bm{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1/2},\ldots,{\bm{\Sigma}}_{M}^{-1/2}\}
.

2.3
High-dimensional heterogeneous tensor mixture model

We focus on third-order tensors
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
in this section. The generalization to higher-order tensors is straightforward.
Assume that there are
K
mixtures of tensor normal distributions with heterogeneous covariances such that

\displaystyle Z\sim\text{Multinomial }(\pi_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,\pi_{K}^{\ast}),

(2)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{X}}|\,Z=k\sim\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k},\,\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{k}),

where
\sum_{k}\pi^{\ast}_{k}=1
,
\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{k}=\left\{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,1},{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,2},{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,3}\right\}
,
k\in[K]
.
In tensor clustering problems,
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
is observable but
Z
is not. Suppose we have
n
unlabeled tensor observations
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
generated independently and identically from the mixture model in (
2
), that is,

\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\pi^{\ast}_{1}\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{1},\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{1})+\cdots+\pi^{\ast}_{K}\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{K},\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{K})

Given
\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}=\{\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\}
, we aim to jointly perform clustering, that is, estimate the cluster label
{\bm{z}}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
, and model estimation, that is, estimate
\pi^{\ast}_{k}
’s,
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}
’s and
\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{k}
’s.

One unique challenge in modeling tensor data is the inherent high-dimensionality of the problem, and it is often imperative to employ effective dimension reduction assumptions that facilitate estimability and interpretability.

Low-rankness on
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}
.
We assume that
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, admits a rank-
R
CP decomposition structure
(
Kolda and Bader 2009
)
, in that,

\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\omega^{\ast}_{k,r}{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,1}\circ{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,2}\circ\bm{\beta}^{\ast}_{k,r,3},

(3)

where
\circ
denotes the outer product,
\omega^{\ast}_{k,r}
is a positive scalar,
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
,
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, and
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}}
,
r\in[R]
. To ensure identifiability,
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,1}
,
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,2}
and
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,3}
are assumed to be unit-norm vectors, that is,
\|{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,m}\|_{2}=1
for all
k,r
and
m
.
The CP low-rank structure is one of the most commonly employed tensor structures
(
Kolda and Bader 2009
)
, and is widely adopted in tensor data analysis, such as medical imaging analysis
(
Zhou et al. 2013
)
, facial image recognition
(
Cao et al. 2014
)
, and recommendation systems
(
Bi et al. 2018
)
.

Internal sparsity on
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}
.

Besides low-rankness, having sparsity in tensor parameters can further reduce the number of free parameters and improve model interpretability
(
Zhou et al. 2023
;
Hao et al. 2021
)
.
Encouraging sparsity by directly adding a penalty on the tensor mean may be computationally infeasible, due to the large number of parameters involved in the penalty term. Alternatively, we consider achieving sparsity under the CP structure.
Specifically, based on (
3
), we assume that
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,m}
’s are sparse.
To differentiate from the usual element-wise sparsity, we refer to the sparsity of
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,m}
’s as the
internal sparsity
of
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}_{k}
.

Separable
\underline{{\bm{\Sigma}}}^{\ast}_{k}
with conditional sparsity.

An attractive feature of the separable covariance structure is that the precision matrix also enjoys a separable structure, that is,

({\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,3}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,2}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,1})^{-1}={{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,3}\otimes{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,2}\otimes{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,1},

(4)

where
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,m}={{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,m}}^{-1}
for all
k,m
.
We assume the separable precision matrices are sparse.
The sparse entries in
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,m}
relate to the conditional dependence between entities along the
m
-th mode in the
k
-th mixture.
Estimating such conditional independence is of interest in many applications. For example, in import-export studies, it is helpful to understand the dependencies across different countries and commodities
(
Leng and Tang 2012
)
.
In (
4
), parameters
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,1}
,
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,2}
and
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,3}
are not identifiable due to scaling, as
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,3}\otimes{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,2}\otimes{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,1}=c_{1}c_{2}{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,3}\otimes\frac{1}{c_{2}}{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,2}\otimes\frac{1}{c_{1}}{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,1}
for any positive constants
c_{1},c_{2}
. To ensure identifiability, we assume
\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,1}\|_{F}=\sqrt{d_{1}}
,
\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,2}\|_{F}=\sqrt{d_{2}}
but let
\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,3}\|_{F}
be unconstrained, that is, we put all additional scaling weights to
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,3}
.
This identifiability condition is unrelated to the incoherence or low-spikeness assumptions
(
Negahban and Wainwright 2012
, e.g.)
, as it does not concern the maximum entry and only focuses on the F-norm. This identifiability condition does not alter the sparsity structures of
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,m}
’s.

Remark 1
.

The two sparsity assumptions on means
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,m}
’s and precisions
{{\bm{\Omega}}}^{\ast}_{k,m}
’s facilitate estimability when the tensor dimensions exceed the sample size. If desired, these two assumptions can be omitted, in which case the ECM algorithm and its theoretical analysis greatly simplify by excluding regularizations.
With only low-rankness in the mean and separability in the covariance, the theoretical results in Section
4
still hold by replacing the sparsity parameters with the respective dimension parameters.

3
High-dimensional ECM estimation

Denote
{\bm{\Theta}}=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{K},{\bm{\theta}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{\theta}}_{K})^{\top}
, where
{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
collects all parameters in the
k
-th mixture with

{\bm{\theta}}_{k}=\left({\bm{\beta}}_{k,1,1}^{\top},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}_{k,1,M}^{\top},\omega_{k,1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}_{k,R,1}^{\top},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}_{k,R,M}^{\top},\omega_{k,R},\mathrm{vec}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,1})^{\top},\ldots,\mathrm{vec}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,M})^{\top}\right)
.
If the true label
{\bm{z}}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
were observed together with
\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}=\{\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\}
, the log-likelihood for the complete data
(\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{z}})
is given by

\ell\left({\bm{\Theta}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{z}}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{1}(z_{i}=k)\left[\log\left(\pi_{k}\right)+\log\left\{f_{k}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\right\}\right],

(5)

where
f_{k}(\cdot)
is defined as in (
1
). When
{\bm{z}}
is unknown, it is common to pose (
5
) as a missing data problem, where the latent label
{\bm{z}}
is treated as missing data.

To estimate
{\bm{\Theta}}
in the presence of missing data, a useful approach is the expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm
(
Dempster et al. 1977
)
, which
encounters a major challenge when applied to our estimation problem.
In the M-step, given
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}
estimated from the previous EM update, one needs to maximize
\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{Z}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}}\left\{\ell({\bm{\Theta}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{Z}})\right\}
with respect to
{\bm{\Theta}}
. This is a challenging problem as the loss function is non-convex and there is no closed-form solution.
To overcome these challenges, we propose a high-dimensional expectation conditional maximization (
HECM
) algorithm that breaks the M-step optimization problem into a sequence of less challenging conditional maximization problems, each of which enjoys a closed-form solution and permits regularization that involves only a fraction of the parameters.

Next, we detail the
HECM
algorithm. Consider the
(t+1)
-th step of the
HECM
iteration.

E-step.
In the E-step, given
\bm{\Theta}^{(t)}
estimated from the previous
HECM
update, we have

\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{P}(z_{i}=k|\,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\frac{\pi_{k}^{(t)}f_{k}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|{\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{(t)})}{\sum_{k}\pi_{k}^{(t)}f_{k}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|{\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{(t)})},\quad k\in[K].

(6)

Next, define
Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{Z}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}}\left\{\ell({\bm{\Theta}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{Z}})\right\}
, which can be written as

Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})\left[\log(\pi_{k})+\log\{f_{k}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\}\right].

(7)

Correspondingly, the objective function in the maximization step can be written as

Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})-\mathcal{P}^{(t+1)}({\bm{\Theta}}),

where
\mathcal{P}^{(t+1)}({\bm{\Theta}})=\sum_{k,r,m}\lambda_{0}^{(t+1)}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}\right\|_{1}+\sum_{k,m}\lambda^{(t+1)}_{m}\left\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}\right\|_{1,\text{off}}
is a penalty term that encourages sparsity in
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}
and
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
for all
k,r
and
m
, and
\lambda^{(t+1)}_{0}
and
\lambda^{(t+1)}_{m}
’s are tuning parameters to be discussed in Section
A
of the supplement.
It is easy to see that the update of
\pi_{k}
can be calculated as

\pi_{k}^{(t+1)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}).

(8)

HCM-step.
The high-dimensional conditional maximization (HCM) step then proceeds by solving the following conditional optimizations.
First, for
k\in[K],r\in[R],m\in[M]
, let

\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}=\arg\max_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}\,Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m},{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}^{(t+1)}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})-\lambda_{0}^{(t+1)}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}\right\|_{1},

(9)

where
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}^{(t+1)}
is
{\bm{\Theta}}
with
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}
removed. In
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}^{(t+1)}
, parameters that are updated before
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}
take values from the
(t+1)
-th step and parameters that are not yet updated take values from the
t
-th step. See Figure
2
for the ordering in conditional updates when
M=3
. The update
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}
can be calculated in closed-form with the
j
-th entry

\tilde{{\bm{\beta}}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}(j)=\begin{cases}{\bm{g}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}(j)-\frac{n\lambda^{(t+1)}_{0}\mathrm{sign}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t)}(j)\right)}{n_{k}^{(t)}C^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m}{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t)}(j,j)}&\mathrm{if}\,|{\bm{h}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}(j)|>\lambda^{(t+1)}_{0},\\
0&\mathrm{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(10)

where
n_{k}^{(t)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
and expressions for
{\bm{g}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}
,
C^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m}
and
{\bm{h}}^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m}
are given in Proposition
2
in the supplement.
The estimate
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}
is then normalized to ensure the unit-norm constraint, and we have
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}=\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}/\|\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}\|_{2}
.

Figure 2:
Parameter updates (left to right) for the
k
-th cluster in the HCM step.

Next, we consider the update of
\omega_{k,r}
’s.
Define operators
\prod\limits^{\circ}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}={\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,1}\circ\cdots\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,M}
and
\prod\limits^{\otimes}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}={\bm{\Omega}}_{k,M}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,1}
.
Let
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\omega_{k,r}}^{(t+1)}
be
{\bm{\Theta}}
with
\omega_{k,r}
removed and parameters updated before and after
\omega_{k,r}
take values from the
(t+1)
-th and
t
-th steps, respectively.
Maximizing
Q_{n}(\omega_{k,r},{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\omega_{k,r}}^{(t+1)}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
with respect to
\omega_{k,r}
is equivalent to solving

\max_{\omega_{k,r}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})\Bigg\|\Big({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{(t+1)}_{i,-r}-\omega_{k,r}\prod\limits^{\circ}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\beta}}^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m}\Big)\times{\underline{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k}^{(t)}}^{1/2}\Bigg\|_{\text{F}}^{2},

where
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{(t+1)}_{i,-r}
is given in Proposition
2
.
Some straightforward algebra yields

\omega_{k,r}^{(t+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})\mathrm{vec}\left({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{(t+1)}_{i,-r}\right)^{\top}\Big(\prod\limits^{\otimes}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\Omega}}^{(t)}_{k,m}\Big)\mathrm{vec}(\prod\limits^{\circ}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\beta}}^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m})}{n_{k}^{(t)}\mathrm{vec}(\prod\limits^{\circ}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\beta}}^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m})^{\top}\Big(\prod\limits^{\otimes}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\Omega}}^{(t)}_{k,m}\Big)\mathrm{vec}(\prod\limits^{\circ}_{m\in[M]}{\bm{\beta}}^{(t+1)}_{k,r,m})}.

(11)

Finally, we consider the update of
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
’s.
Let
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}^{(t+1)}
be
{\bm{\Theta}}
with
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
removed and parameters updated before and after
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
take values from the
(t+1)
-th and
t
-th steps, respectively.
We consider, for
k\in[K],m\in[M]
,

\tilde{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}=\arg\max_{{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}\,Q_{n}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m},{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}^{(t+1)}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})-\lambda^{(t+1)}_{m}\left\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}\right\|_{1,\text{off}}.

With some straightforward algebra, the above objective function can be written as

\tilde{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}=\arg\min_{{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}\,\frac{n_{k}^{(t)}}{d_{m}n}\left\{-\log\left|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}\right|+\text{tr}({\bm{S}}^{(t+1)}_{k,m}{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m})\right\}+\lambda^{(t+1)}_{m}\left\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}\right\|_{1,\text{off}},

(12)

where
{\bm{S}}^{(t+1)}_{k,m}=\frac{d_{m}}{dn_{k}^{(t)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})\bar{\bm{X}}^{(t+1)}_{i,k,m}{{}\bar{\bm{X}}^{(t+1)}_{i,k,m}}^{\top}
,
\bar{\bm{X}}^{(t+1)}_{i,k,m}=\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{(t+1)}\right)_{(m)}\bm{A}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
and
\bm{A}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}=\left({\prod\limits^{\otimes}_{m^{\prime}>m}{\bm{\Omega}}^{(t)}_{k,m^{\prime}}}\otimes{\prod\limits^{\otimes}_{m^{\prime}<m}{\bm{\Omega}}^{(t+1)}_{k,m^{\prime}}}\right)^{1/2}
.
The optimization problem in (
12
) is convex and
can be solved using the GLasso algorithm
(
Friedman et al. 2008
)
.
To satisfy the identifiability constraint
\left\|{\bm{\Omega}}^{\ast}_{k,m}\right\|_{\text{F}}/\sqrt{d_{m}}=1
for
m\leq M-1
,
\tilde{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
is first normalized such that
\check{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}=\sqrt{d_{m}}\tilde{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}/\|\tilde{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}\|_{\text{F}}
for all
m
.
We then define

{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}=\begin{cases}\check{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)},&m\leq M-1,\\
\eta_{k}^{(t+1)}\check{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,M}^{(t+1)},&m=M,\end{cases}

(13)

where
\eta_{k}^{(t+1)}
, the scalar collecting weights from all
M
modes, is calculated by maximizing the M-step objective function calculated using
\eta_{k}{\check{\bm{\Omega}}}^{(t+1)}_{k,M}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\check{\bm{\Omega}}}^{(t+1)}_{k,2}\otimes{\check{\bm{\Omega}}}^{(t+1)}_{k,1}
with respect to
\eta_{k}
, and is multiplied to the last mode. The formula of
\eta_{k}^{(t+1)}
is provided in Section
A
.

The above estimation procedure is summarized in Algorithm
1
.
In practice, to speed up convergence, one may repeat steps 2.2-2.3 several times before exiting the HCM step. Such a heuristic procedure may reduce the number of steps needed to reach convergence in our experiments.
Given the estimated
\hat{\tau}_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})
from the last
HECM
iterate, we estimate the class labels using

\hat{\bm{z}}_{i}=\arg\max_{k}\hat{\tau}_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}),\quad i\in[n].

We discuss the initialization, stopping rule and parameter tuning in Section
A
.

Algorithm 1

The
HECM
Algorithm for Heterogeneous Tensor Mixture Model

Input
: data
\{\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, number of clusters
K
, rank
R
, maximum number of iterations
T
and tuning parameters
\lambda^{(t)}_{0},\{\lambda_{m}^{(t)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
,
t\in[T]
.

Initialization
: calculate
\pi_{k}^{(0)}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(0)}
,
\omega_{k,r}^{(0)}
and
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(0)}
, for all
k,r
and
m
.

Repeat
the following steps for
t\in[T]
,

1. E-step
: compute
\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
using (
6
) for all
i
and
k
,

2. HCM-step
:

2.1:
update
\pi_{k}^{(t+1)}
using
(\ref{pi})
for all
k
;

2.2:
update
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}^{(t+1)}
using (
10
) and set
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}=\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}/\|\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}\|_{2}
; update
\omega_{k,r}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\omega_{k,r}}^{(t+1)}
using (
11
) for all
k,r
and
m
;

2.3:
update
\tilde{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}^{(t+1)}
using (
12
) and set
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
using (
13
) for all
k
and
m
;

Stop
if the algorithm has converged.

Output
: Cluster label
\hat{\bm{z}}
, cluster mean
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{k}
and precision matrices
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
.

Connection to existing EM-type algorithms.

Compared with the standard EM algorithm, the HCM-step in Algorithm
1
does not find
\arg\max_{{\bm{\Theta}}}\,Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
, which is an intractable non-convex optimization under our setting.
Instead, the ECM step gives solutions to a sequence of conditional optimization problems
\arg\max_{\bm{\vartheta}}Q_{n}(\bm{\vartheta},{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\bm{\vartheta}}^{(t+1)}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
, each of which is convex and easy to solve.
Due to this distinction, existing techniques
(
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Hao et al. 2017
)
that analyze EM iterates, in fixed or high dimensions, assuming
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t+1)}=\arg\max_{{\bm{\Theta}}}Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
are not directly applicable.
The
HECM
algorithm is an instance of the expectation-conditional-maximization (ECM) algorithm
(
Meng and Rubin 1993
;
Meng 1994
)
. Convergence of the ECM algorithm to some arbitrary fixed point has been studied
(
Meng 1994
)
; however, to our knowledge, its convergence to the unknown true parameter has not been investigated, even in the low-dimensional regime. This analysis turns out to be highly challenging due to the dual nonconvexity nature from both the EM-type estimation and the objective function in the M-step; see Section
4.2
.

4
Theoretical analysis

This section establishes statistical guarantees for the local convergence of the
HECM
estimator. We first develop theory when rank
R=1

and then generalize our results to the more challenging case of rank
R>1
. All proofs are collected in the supplement.

Let
{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}
denote the true parameters located in a non-empty compact convex set. Define
\omega_{\max}=\max_{k,r}\omega_{k,r}^{\ast}
,
\omega_{\min}=\min_{k,r}\omega_{k,r}^{\ast}
and
d_{\max}=\max_{m\in[M]}d_{m}
.
Denote the sparsity parameters for the tensor mean and the precision matrices as
s_{1}=\max\limits_{k,r,m}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}\|_{0}
and
s_{2}=\max\limits_{k,m}\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}\|_{0,\text{off}}
, respectively.
Without loss of generality, we assume
\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}/\sqrt{d_{m}}=1
for all
k
and
m
.
Define the normalized distance metric (noting
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}\|=1
)

\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})=\max\limits_{k,r,m}\left\{\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}\|_{2},\frac{|\omega_{k,r}-\omega_{k,r}^{\ast}|}{|\omega_{k,r}^{\ast}|},\frac{\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}}{\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}}\right\}

(14)

and let
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
denote the ball around
{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}
with
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\leq\alpha
.
Next, we introduce several regularity conditions common for both
R=1
and
R>1
.

Condition 1
.

Assume
\min_{k}\pi^{\ast}_{k}>r_{0}
for some constant
r_{0}>0
,
\max_{k}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\max}=O(1)
and
\omega_{\max}/\omega_{\min}=O(1)
. Furthermore, assume there exist some positive constants
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
such that
\phi_{1}\leq\sigma_{\min}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast})\leq\sigma_{\max}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast})\leq\phi_{2}
, for
k\in[K]
,
m\in[M]
.

The condition
\max_{k}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\max}=O(1)
, which bounds the tensor mean element-wisely, is needed to control errors from estimating the mean, which in turn regulates errors from estimating the precision matrices; we refer to
Wu and Yang 2020
for more discussions.
The condition
\omega_{\max}/\omega_{\min}=O(1)
holds in the majority of work on vector mixture models, including
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Wang et al. 2016
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
;
Doss et al. 2020
;
Kwon et al. 2021
and stipulates that weights

\omega_{k,1}^{\ast},\ldots,\omega_{k,R}^{\ast}
are of the same order, as commonly done in the tensor clustering literature
(
Sun and Li 2019
, e.g.,)
.
Lastly, the bounded eigenvalue condition on the precision matrices is a regularity condition that has been employed in the literature
(
Leng and Tang 2012
;
Lyu et al. 2019
)
.

Condition 2
.

The initial values
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(0)}
,
\omega_{k,r}^{(0)}
,
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(0)}
for all
k,r
and
m
satisfy

\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\left(\frac{C_{0}\omega_{\min}}{(R-1)\omega_{\max}}\right)^{\frac{1}{M-1}}\right\},

where
C_{0}\in[0,1/3]
is a positive constant depending on
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
and
\frac{\|{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(0)}-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}\|_{2}}{\sigma_{\min}({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast})}\leq 1/2
.

This condition requires the initial values to be reasonably close to the true parameters. Such an initial error condition is commonly considered in the non-convex optimization literature
(
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Mai et al. 2021
)
. When
R=1
, the bound on
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
is reduced to
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\leq\frac{1}{2}
, which is comparable to the initial condition employed in
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
.
Assuming
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\leq\frac{1}{2}
is a mild condition as
{\bm{\beta}}^{(0)}_{k,r,m}
’s and
{\bm{\beta}}^{\ast}_{k,r,m}
’s are normalized to have a unit norm and

\omega_{k,r}
,
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
are both normalized in
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
.
When
R>1
, the constant
\left(\frac{C_{0}\omega_{\min}}{(R-1)\omega_{\max}}\right)^{\frac{1}{M-1}}
is less than
1/2
as
C_{0}\in[0,1/3]
, leading to a stronger initial condition.

The next condition generalizes the signal-to-noise condition in
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
, which considers a vector Gaussian mixture model with known isotropic covariances. Recall that
\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})=\mathbb{P}(z_{i}=k|\,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i},{\bm{\Theta}})
.
With some straightforward algebra (see Section
E6
), the derivative of
\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})
with respect to
({\bm{\theta}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{\theta}}_{K})
can be written as

\nabla_{{\bm{\theta}}_{l}}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})\tau_{il}({\bm{\Theta}})J_{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{l}),&\text{for }l\neq k,\\
\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})\{1-\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})\}J_{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),&\text{for }l=k,\end{array}\right.

and the specific form of
J_{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{l})
is given in Lemma
S14
due to space limitations.

Condition 3

(Separability Condition)
.

For
{\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\left\{W_{ikl}\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}})\tau_{il}({\bm{\Theta}})\right\}^{2}\leq\frac{\gamma^{2}}{24^{2}K^{4}(R+1)^{4}(M+1)^{2}},\quad l\neq k\in[K],

(15)

where
W_{ikl}
is a function of
J_{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{k})
,
J_{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{l})
as in (
A67
) and
\gamma>0
is a sufficiently small separability parameter.

Condition
3
stipulates that the clusters are sufficiently separated and the probability that a data point belongs to two different clusters cannot be both large.
Consider a vector Gaussian mixture model with
\bm{X}\sim\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}}^{\ast},\sigma^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast},\sigma^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d})
, where
{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
d
is fixed. Then, as stated in Proposition
1
, Condition
3
holds under the common signal-to-noise ratio condition that requires
\frac{\left\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\right\|_{2}}{\sigma}
to be sufficiently large (see, for example,
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
).

Proposition 1
.

Assume
\bm{X}\sim\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}}^{\ast},\sigma^{2}\bm{1}_{d})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast},\sigma^{2}\bm{1}_{d})
and
{\bm{\mu}}\in\{{\bm{\mu}}\,|\,\|{\bm{\mu}}-{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}{\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\|_{2}}\}
as in
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
.
When
\frac{\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\|_{2}}{\sigma}
is lower bounded by a sufficiently large constant, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\left\{W_{i21}\tau_{i1}({\bm{\mu}})\tau_{i2}({\bm{\mu}})\right\}^{2}\leq 2\exp\left\{4\log\left(\frac{\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\|_{2}}{\sigma}\right)-4\frac{\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right\},

where
W_{i21}
,
\tau_{i1}({\bm{\mu}})
and
\tau_{i2}({\bm{\mu}})
are as defined in (
15
).

It is seen from Proposition
1
that
\mathbb{E}\left\{W_{i12}\tau_{i1}({\bm{\mu}})\tau_{i2}({\bm{\mu}})\right\}^{2}
can be made sufficiently small if the signal-to-noise ratio
\frac{\left\|{\bm{\mu}}^{\ast}\right\|_{2}}{\sigma}
is sufficiently large.

4.1
Theory with rank
R=1

We analyze a sample-splitting version of the
HECM
algorithm in Algorithm
2
, similar to other work on EM algorithms
(
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
)
.
In Algorithm
2
, we divide the
n
samples into
T
subsets of size
\lfloor n/T\rfloor
and use a fresh subset of samples in each iteration; see also remarks after Theorem
1
.

Condition 4
.

The sample size
n_{0}=n/T
satisfies

n_{0}\succsim\max\left\{\frac{s_{1}\log d}{\omega_{\min}^{2}},\,\,(s_{2}+d_{\max})\log d\right\}.

(16)

The first term
{s_{1}\log d}/{\omega_{\min}^{2}}
in the sample complexity lower bound is related to estimating the low-rank and sparse tensor means while the second term
(s_{2}+d_{\max})\log d
is related to estimating the sparse separable precision matrices.

Algorithm 2

The
HECM
Algorithm with Sample Splitting

Input
: data
\{\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, number of clusters
K
, rank
R
, maximum number of iterations
T
and tuning parameters
\lambda^{(t)}_{0},\{\lambda_{m}^{(t)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
,
t\in[T]
.

Initialization
: calculate
\pi_{k}^{(0)}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(0)}
,
\omega_{k,r}^{(0)}
and
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(0)}
, for all
k,r
and
m
. Split the dataset into
T
subsets with sample size
n_{0}=n/T
, which is assumed to be integer.

Repeat
the following steps for
t\in[T]
,

1. E-step
: using the
t
th data split, compute
\tau_{ik}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})
using (
6
) for all
i
and
k
,

2. HCM-step
: using the
t
th data split,

2.1:
update
\pi_{k}^{(t+1)}
using
(\ref{pi})
for all
k
;

2.2:
update
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}}^{(t+1)}
using (
10
) and set
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}=\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}/\|\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{(t+1)}\|_{2}
; update
\omega_{k,r}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-\omega_{k,r}}^{(t+1)}
using (
11
) for all
k,r
and
m
;

2.3:
update
\tilde{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
given
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}^{(t+1)}
using (
12
) and set
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{(t+1)}
using (
13
) for all
k
and
m
;

Stop
if the algorithm has converged.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose Conditions
1
-
4
hold with
\gamma d_{\max}\leq C_{1}
for some constant
C_{1}>0
.
Let

\lambda_{0}^{(t)}=4\epsilon_{0}+\tau_{0}\frac{\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t-1)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}{\sqrt{s_{1}}},\quad\lambda_{m}^{(t)}=4\epsilon_{m}+3\tau_{1}\frac{\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t-1)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}{2\sqrt{s_{2}+d_{m}}},

(17)

where
\tau_{0}=C_{\tau}\gamma
,
\tau_{1}=d\tau_{0}
,
d=\prod_{m}d_{m}
,
\epsilon_{0}=c_{1}\omega_{\max}\sqrt{T\log d/n}
and
\epsilon_{m}=c_{2}({d}/{d_{m}})\sqrt{T\log d/n}
for some constants
C_{\tau},c_{1},c_{2}>0
.
The estimator
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}
from the
t
-th iteration of Algorithm
2
satisfies with probability
1-o(1)
,

\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\\
\leq\underbrace{\rho^{t}\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}_{\text{computational error}}+\frac{C_{2}}{1-\rho}\underbrace{\left\{\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}}_{\text{statistical error}},

(18)

where
C_{2}>0
is a constant, the contraction parameter
\rho
given in (
A82
) satisfies
0<\rho\leq 1/3
and the maximum number of iterations
T\precsim(-\log\rho)^{-1}\log(d_{\max}n\cdot\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}))\precsim\log d
.

Several important implications are provided as follows.

Computational error and statistical error trade-off.
The non-asymptotic error bound in (
18
) involves two terms, the first of which is the computational error and it decreases geometrically in the iteration number
t
, whereas the second term is the statistical error and is independent of
t
.
Thus, the
HECM
iterates are guaranteed to converge geometrically to a neighborhood that is within statistical precision of the unknown true parameter.
When the iteration
t
reaches its maximum
T
, the computation error is dominated by the statistical error and the algorithm can be terminated.

Statistical errors.
Considering the statistical error, apart from the term
T
which satisfies
T\precsim\log\,d
, the first error term
\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{{s_{1}\log d}/{n}}
is related to estimating the low-rank and sparse tensor means, which matches with the optimal rate
\sqrt{s\log d/n_{0}}
in high-dimensional models with sparsity parameter
s
, dimension
d
and sample size
n_{0}

(
Wainwright 2019
)
, and the second error term
\max_{m}\sqrt{{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}/(nd_{m})}
is related to estimating the sparse tensor precisions. The sample splitting scheme uses a fresh subset of the data at each iteration and it is a technique commonly considered in analyzing EM algorithms
(
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
)
.
In Theorem
1
, it is seen that the iteration number
T
does not affect the computational error though it increases the statistical error by at most a factor of
\log d
. We expect this logarithm factor can be eliminated by directly analyzing Algorithm
1
, which however incurs significant technical complexity as it requires the statistical error bound in Lemma
3b
to hold uniformly over
\mathcal{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
.

The regularization parameters can be alternatively written as

\lambda_{0}^{(t+1)}=\rho\lambda_{0}^{(t)}+\Delta_{0}
, with
\Delta_{0}=C_{0}^{\prime}\tau_{0}\left\{\max\left\{\omega_{\max},\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}\sqrt{s_{1}}}\right\}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}
, and

\lambda_{m}^{(t+1)}=\rho\lambda_{m}^{(t)}+\Delta_{m}
,
with
\Delta_{m}=C_{m}^{\prime}\tau_{1}\left\{\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}
, for some constants
C_{0}^{\prime},C_{m}^{\prime}>0
. This is similar to the result from
Yi and Caramanis 2015
.
In practice, parameters such as
\rho
,
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
\tau_{0}
,
\omega_{\min}
and
\omega_{\max}
are based on the true model and are unknown. Instead, one can estimate these parameters or tune
\lambda^{(t)}_{0}
and
\lambda^{(t)}_{m}
’s at each iteration; see Section
A
.

4.2
Proof outline and key technical challenges

As
HECM
cannot access the maximizer of
Q_{n}(\cdot|\cdot)
in the M-step, existing arguments and techniques in the population and sample-based analysis of the standard EM algorithms
(
Yi and Caramanis 2015
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
)
are not directly applicable.
To put our discussions in context, we first give a brief review of the population and sample-based analysis of the standard EM algorithm, which utilizes properties of the sample function
Q_{n}(\cdot|\cdot)
and population function
Q(\cdot|\cdot)
.
Specifically, the contraction of EM iterates is established using several key results including a strong concavity condition stipulating that
Q(\cdot|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
is strongly concave, that is,

Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime\prime}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})-Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})-\langle\nabla Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}),{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime\prime}-{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}\rangle\leq-\gamma_{n}\|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime\prime}-{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}\|^{2},

(19)

where
\gamma_{n}\geq 0
, a gradient stability condition with
\tau\geq 0

\|\nabla Q({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}})-\nabla Q({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\|_{2}\leq\tau\|{\bm{\Theta}}-{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}\|_{2},

(20)

and a statistical error condition quantifying the difference

\|\nabla Q_{n}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}|{\bm{\Theta}})-\nabla Q({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}|{\bm{\Theta}})\|_{\mathcal{P}},

(21)

for some norm
\mathcal{P}
.
Our analysis of the ECM algorithm requires considering a sequence of conditional
Q
functions including
Q({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})
,
Q(\omega_{k}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-\omega_{k}}|{\bm{\Theta}})
and
Q({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})
for all
k
,
m
.
For example, regarding the update of
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}
, the sample conditional
Q
function is expressed as
Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})
, where
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime}
is the parameter to be updated and
\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}
collects all other parameters being conditioned on, with some already updated and some yet to be updated.
Computational and statistical properties of the conditional
Q
and
Q_{n}
functions thus need to be established uniformly over all
\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
for some
\alpha>0
.

Specifically, we establish in Lemma
1b
that

\displaystyle Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})-Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})-\left\langle\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}}),{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime\prime}-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime\prime}-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2},

for
{\bm{\Theta}},\bar{\bm{\Theta}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
. Compared to (
19
),
this is a much stronger condition, as it holds for all
{\bm{\Theta}},\bar{\bm{\Theta}}\in\mathcal{B}
in a neighborhood of
{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}
. This stronger condition is also established in Lemma
1b
for
\omega_{k}
’s and
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}
’s.
Next, in Lemma
2b
we demonstrate that the following stability condition holds uniformly over all
\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
,

\left\lVert\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})-\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\tau_{0}\cdot\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}),

where
\tau_{0}>0
,
{\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
and
\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
is defined as in (
14
).
This is a much stronger condition than (
20
), involving
{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime},{\bm{\Theta}},\bar{\bm{\Theta}}
and
{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}
.
The statistical error shown in Lemma
3b
quantifies

\left\|\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})-\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\prime},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}})\right\|_{\mathcal{P}}
uniformly over
\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
for
{\bm{\Theta}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
, which is again a stronger result than (
21
). Finally, utilizing these computational and statistical properties of the conditional
Q
functions, Lemma
4b
establishes a critical result that ensures contraction after one
HECM
update.

Unlike analysis of the standard EM algorithm, our one-step contraction result requires carefully balancing the maximizer of a sequence of conditional
Q
functions.
Specifically, a critical property in
Hao et al. 2017
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
is the self-consistency property of the population
Q
function, that is,

{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}=\arg\max_{{\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}}Q({\bm{\Theta}}^{\prime}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}).

(22)

This is crucial in establishing various properties in the population-level analysis, and in finally showing the one-step contraction in the sample-level analysis.
However, our analysis cannot take advantage of the self-consistency property in (
22
).
Due to the conditional nature of our parameter updates, we need to precisely characterize
\nabla_{{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}Q({\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}^{\ast},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
,
\nabla_{\omega_{k}}Q(\omega_{k}^{\ast},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-\omega_{k}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
and
\nabla_{{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}Q({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast},\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}_{-{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
for all
k
,
m
and
\bar{{\bm{\Theta}}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}({\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})
; see proofs in Section
D4
. Finally, the low-rank structure of the tensor mean and separable covariance structure pose additional technical challenges in the theoretical analysis. For example, both the low-rank decomposition and separable covariance decomposition require normalization steps to ensure identifiability. These normalization procedures introduce additional complexities in the proof.

4.3
Theory with rank
R>1

The theoretical analysis of
R>1
is more challenging as components from different rank are generally not orthogonal. To quantify the correlation between decomposed components
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}
’s across different ranks, we define the following incoherence parameter

\xi=\max_{k,\,r^{\prime}\neq r,\,m}\left|\left\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r^{\prime},m}^{\ast},{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}\right\rangle\right|.

(23)

For example, when
\xi=0
, the components
{\bm{\beta}}_{k,r,m}^{\ast}
’s are orthogonal (as they are unit-norm vectors). In our theoretical analysis, we impose an upper bound condition on
\xi
that allows the decomposed components to be correlated but only to a certain degree, similar to

(
Anandkumar et al. 2014a
;
Sun and Li 2019
;
Cai et al. 2020
;
Xia et al. 2021
)
.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose Conditions
1
-
4
hold with
\gamma d_{\max}\leq C_{1}
and
R\xi^{M}\precsim(\log d)^{-1}
, where
C_{1}
is as defined in Theorem
1
.
Let

\lambda_{0}^{(t)}=4\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}+\tau^{\prime}_{0}\frac{\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t-1)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}{\sqrt{s_{1}}},\quad\lambda_{m}^{(t)}=4\epsilon_{m}+3\tau_{1}\frac{\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t-1)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}{2\sqrt{s_{2}+d_{m}}},

where
\tau^{\prime}_{0}=C^{\prime}_{\tau}\gamma
,
\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}=c^{\prime}_{1}\omega_{\max}\sqrt{T\log d/n}
for some constants
C^{\prime}_{\tau},c^{\prime}_{1}>0
, and
\tau_{1}
and
\epsilon_{m}
are as defined in (
17
).
The estimator
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}
from the
t
-th iteration of Algorithm
2
satisfies with probability
1-o(1)
,

\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\\
\leq\underbrace{\rho_{R}^{t}\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})}_{\text{computational error}}+\frac{C^{\prime}_{2}}{1-\rho_{R}}\underbrace{\left\{\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}}_{\text{statistical error}},

(24)

where
C^{\prime}_{2}>0
is a constant,
\rho_{R}
given in (
A91
) satisfies
\rho\leq\rho_{R}\leq 1/2
with
\rho
in (
18
), and the maximum number of iterations
T\precsim(-\log\rho_{R})^{-1}\log(d_{\max}n\cdot\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}))\precsim\log d
.

Similar to Theorem
1
, when the number of iterations
t
reaches
T
, the computational error will be dominated by the statistical error, leading to

\rho_{R}^{t}\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\precsim\frac{1}{1-\rho_{R}}\left\{\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}
.
Compared to Theorem
1
, it is seen that the contraction parameter
\rho_{R}
is bounded below by
\rho
, which indicates that the contraction rate can be slower in the general rank case. When the number of iterations
t
reaches
T
, the computational error will be dominated by the statistical error, leading to
\rho_{R}^{t}\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast})\precsim\frac{1}{1-\rho_{R}}\left\{\frac{1}{\omega_{\min}}\sqrt{T\frac{s_{1}\log d}{n}}+\max_{m}\sqrt{T\frac{(s_{2}+d_{m})\log d}{nd_{m}}}\right\}
. Correspondingly, more iterations are needed to reach convergence as
(-\log\rho_{R})^{-1}\log(d_{\max}n\cdot\textrm{D}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(0)},{\bm{\Theta}}^{\ast}))
is larger. This agrees with the expectation that, as the tensor recovery problem becomes more challenging, the algorithm has a slower convergence rate. Consider the condition
R\xi^{M}\leq C(\log d)^{-1}
under a simple case where
d_{1}=d_{2}=\dots=d_{M}
. It holds that
d=d_{1}^{M}
and the condition can be written as

M\xi^{M}\leq\frac{C}{R\log(d_{1})}
. Since
M\xi^{M}
is an increasing function in
M
for
M\geq 1
, a larger
M
requires a stronger condition on
\xi
to satisfy the incoherence assumption. This type of condition on rank is common in the literature. Suppose all dimensions are equal and tensor components are sampled from the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{S}^{d_{1}-1}
,
Anandkumar et al. 2014b
showed that
\xi\lesssim\sqrt{\log(R)/d_{1}}
with high probability. The condition in Theorem
2
then becomes
R\log(R)^{M/2}\preceq\frac{d_{1}^{M/2}}{\log(d)}
, which is comparable with the condition in
Anandkumar et al. 2014b
.

5
Over-specification and low signal-to-noise ratio

In this section, we consider two important issues in fitting an ECM algorithm to tensor mixture models: over-specification of the number of clusters and low signal-to-noise ratio. We present theoretical results and discuss their practical implications.

5.1
Over-specified mixtures

We aim to understand the behavior of the ECM algorithm in over-specified tensor mixture models.
To simplify our analysis, we consider the isotropic case, similar as in
Dwivedi et al. 2020
, and assume
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}_{T}({\bf 0},\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{d_{1}},{\bf I}_{d_{2}},{\bf I}_{d_{3}}\})
, where
\sigma^{2}
is known.
We fit the data using a symmetric two-component tensor normal mixture with known mixture weights:

\pi\times\mathcal{N}_{T}(-\bm{\mathcal{U}},\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{d_{1}},{\bf I}_{d_{2}},{\bf I}_{d_{3}}\})+(1-\pi)\times\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bm{\mathcal{U}},\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{d_{1}},{\bf I}_{d_{2}},{\bf I}_{d_{3}}\}),

(25)

where
\bm{\mathcal{U}}=\omega{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{3}
. In this case, model (
25
) over-specifies the number of mixtures.

Denote
{\bm{\Theta}}=({\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{\top},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}^{\top},{\bm{\beta}}_{3}^{\top},\omega)^{\top}
and
\underline{{\bf I}_{\sigma}}=\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{d_{1}},{\bf I}_{d_{2}},{\bf I}_{d_{3}}\}
. In the
(t+1)
-th step of the ECM iteration, given
{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}
from the previous ECM update, the E-step calculates

\tau_{i}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{P}(z_{i}=1|\,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\frac{\pi f(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)},\underline{{\bf I}_{\sigma}})}{\pi f(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)},\underline{{\bf I}_{\sigma}})+(1-\pi)f(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}|-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)},\underline{{\bf I}_{\sigma}})}.

(26)

Next, define
Q({\bm{\Theta}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{E}_{Z|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}}\{\ell({\bm{\Theta}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{z}})\}
, where
\ell({\bm{\Theta}}|\underline{\bm{\mathcal{X}}},{\bm{z}})
is calculated based on (
26
).
In the CM-step, we consider conditional updates

\tilde{{\bm{\beta}}}_{m}^{(t+1)}=\arg\max_{{\bm{\beta}}_{m}}Q_{n}({\bm{\beta}}_{m},{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t+1)}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{m}}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)}),

(27)

where
{\bm{\Theta}}_{-{\bm{\beta}}_{m}}^{(t+1)}
is defined similarly as in (
9
).
The update of
\tilde{{\bm{\beta}}}_{m}^{(t+1)}
’s can be calculated in closed-forms. For example,

\tilde{{\bm{\beta}}}_{1}^{(t+1)}=\frac{1}{n\omega^{(t)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\left\{2\tau_{i}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})-1\right\}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i})_{(1)}\right]\mathrm{vec}({\bm{\beta}}_{2}^{(t)}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{3}^{(t)})
.
After computing
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{m}^{(t+1)}
’s, we normalize it as
{\bm{\beta}}_{m}^{(t+1)}=\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{m}^{(t+1)}/\|\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{m}^{(t+1)}\|_{2}
and update
\omega
via

\displaystyle\omega^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\arg\max_{\omega}Q_{n}(\omega,{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t+1)}_{-\omega}|{\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})

(28)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{2\tau_{i}({\bm{\Theta}}^{(t)})-1\right\}\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i})^{\top}\mathrm{vec}({\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{(t+1)}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{2}^{(t+1)}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{3}^{(t+1)}).

Our theoretical analysis considers the balanced case with
\pi=1/2
and unbalanced case with
\pi\neq\frac{1}{2}
separately, as they give distinct convergence rates.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose we fit a balanced case (i.e.,
\pi=\frac{1}{2}
) of the mixture model (
25
) using an ECM algorithm as in (
26
)-(
28
). There exist positive constants
C_{3},C_{4}
such that for any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, any sample size
n\geq C_{3}(\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log(\log(1/\alpha)/\delta))
, the estimator from the
t
-th iteration satisfies with probability
1-\delta
,

\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\leq\underbrace{\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}\gamma_{p}(\omega^{(j)})}_{\text{computational error}}+\underbrace{C_{4}\sigma\left(\sigma^{2}\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log\left(\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{\delta}\right)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}-\alpha}}_{\text{statistical error}},

(29)

where
\gamma_{p}(\omega)=p+\frac{1-p}{1+\omega^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}
with

p\approx 0.92
and the iterate number
t\succsim\log\left(\frac{n\omega^{(0)}}{\sigma^{2}\sum_{m}d_{m}}\right)+\left(\frac{n}{\sum_{m}d_{m}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha}\log(1/\alpha)\log\left(\frac{n}{\sum_{m}d_{m}}\right)
.

The proof is given in Section
C4
, where we also summarize the key technical challenges. One salient feature of the convergence rate under this over-specified setting is that the contraction rate
\gamma_{p}(\omega)
is not globally bounded away from 1, and in fact
\gamma_{p}(\omega)\rightarrow 1
as
\omega\rightarrow 0
. In the ECM iterates,
\gamma_{p}(\omega^{(j)})
is calculated as a function of
\omega^{(j)}=\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(j)}\|_{F}
, which approaches 0 as
j
increases (the true weight
\omega^{*}=0
). This is in sharp contrast with results under the well-specified setting in Theorems
1
-
2
.
In addition, Theorem
3
shows that the ECM iterates converge to the true parameter from an arbitrary initialization. However, the rate of convergence changes as a function of the distance of the current iterate to the true parameter value, and it becomes exponentially slower as the iterates approach the true parameter. This observation has also been made for vector mixture models
(
Dwivedi et al. 2020
)
.

As shown in our proof, once the iteration number
t
satisfies the lower
bound stated in the theorem, the statistical error dominates the computational error. The parameter
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
can be chosen arbitrarily close to 0. Hence, at the expense of increasing the lower bound on the number of iterations by
\log(1/\alpha)
, we can obtain statistical error rates arbitrarily close to
(\sum_{m}d_{m}/n)^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
Compared to
Dwivedi et al. 2020
, where the convergence rate is a polynomial of the vector dimension
d
, our convergence rate depends on
\sum_{m}d_{m}
instead of
\prod_{m}d_{m}
, dimension of the vectorized tensor. This improvement in rate is due to the consideration of tensor low-rank structure.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose we fit an unbalanced case (i.e.,
\pi\neq\frac{1}{2}
) of the mixture model (
25
) using an ECM algorithm as in (
26
)-(
28
). There exist positive constants
C_{5},C_{6}
such that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and
sample size
n\geq C_{5}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\rho^{4}}\left(\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log\left(1/\delta\right)\right)
, the estimator from the
t
-th iteration satisfies with probability
1-\delta
,

\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\leq\underbrace{\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\left(1-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\right)^{t}}_{\text{computational error}}+\underbrace{\frac{C_{6}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\sigma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log\left(1/\delta\right)}{n}}}_{\text{statistical error}}

(30)

where
\rho=|1-2\pi|\in(0,1)
.

Theorem
4
provides a non-asymptotic error bound for the ECM estimator in the unbalanced case. Unlike in the balanced case, the contraction rate is globally upper bounded away from 1.
The bound in (
30
) shows that the level of unbalancedness plays a critical role in the rate of convergence for the ECM algorithm. When the mixtures become more balanced, that is, as
\pi
approaches
1/2
, the contraction rate approaches 1. This is also observed in
Dwivedi et al. 2020
, where they investigated numerically the loglikelihood computed under the over-specified model and found that it has more curvature under the unbalanced case and is very flat near the origin under the balanced case.

Similar to Theorem
3
, the parameter
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
can be chosen arbitrarily close to 0. Hence, we can obtain statistically error rates arbitrarily close to
(\sum_{m}d_{m}/n)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Compared to
Dwivedi et al. 2020
, where the convergence rate is a polynomial of the vector dimension
d
, our convergence rate depends on
\sum_{m}d_{m}
instead of
\prod_{m}d_{m}
, dimension of the vectorized tensor. This improvement in rate is again due to the consideration of tensor low-rank structure.

5.2
Low signal-to-noise ratio

Many existing works on EM algorithms assume that the clusters are well separated (high signal-to-noise ratio)
(
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
, e.g.)
. In our analysis in Theorems
1
-
2
, we adopted a similar assumption in Condition
3
. Recently,
Kwon et al. 2021
established the non-asymptotic behavior of the standard EM algorithm when the high signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) condition fails. Building upon the ideas presented in
Kwon et al. 2021
, we conduct a theoretical study of our proposed ECM algorithm in the two-component tensor mixture model under low SNR conditions. Specifically, we assume
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
are from a balanced symmetric tensor mixture

\frac{1}{2}\times\mathcal{N}_{T}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast},\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}_{d_{1}},{\bf I}_{d_{2}},{\bf I}_{d_{3}}\})+\frac{1}{2}\times\mathcal{N}_{T}(-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast},\{\sigma^{2}{\bf I}^{\ast},{\bf I}^{\ast},{\bf I}^{\ast}\}),

(31)

where
\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}=\omega^{\ast}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{\ast}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{2}^{\ast}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{3}^{\ast}
.
We consider a low SNR setting, characterized by the condition that
\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}\leq C_{0}\epsilon
, with
\epsilon=\left(\sigma^{2}\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log\left(\log\left(1/\alpha\right)/\delta\right)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}-\alpha}
for
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose we fit (
31
) with
\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}\leq C_{0}\epsilon
using an ECM algorithm as in (
26
)-(
28
). There exist positive constants
C_{7},C_{8}
such that for any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\omega^{(0)}<\sqrt{2}\sigma
, any sample size
n\geq C_{7}(\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log(\log(1/\alpha)/\delta))
, the estimator from the
t
-th iteration satisfies with probability
1-\delta
,

\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(t)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\leq\underbrace{\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(0)}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{F}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}\left(1-\frac{2{\omega^{(j)}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)}_{\text{computational error}}+\underbrace{C_{8}\sigma\left(\sigma^{2}\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}+\log\left(\frac{\log\left(1/\alpha\right)}{\delta}\right)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}-\alpha}}_{\text{statistical error }},

where the iterate number
t\succsim\left(\frac{n}{\sum_{m}d_{m}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha}\log(1/\alpha)\log\left(\frac{n}{\sum_{m}d_{m}}\right)
.

The proof is given in Section
C5
, where we also summarize the key technical challenges. It is seen that under the low SNR setting, the contraction rate
1-2{\omega^{(j)}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}
is not bounded away from 1. In the ECM iterates,
\omega^{(j)}=\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{(j)}\|_{F}
can be very close to 0, as the true signal
\omega^{\ast}=\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\ast}\|_{\text{F}}\leq C_{0}\epsilon
. This is in sharp contrast with results under the high SNR setting in Theorems
1
-
2
.
Theorem
5
shows that the ECM iterates converge to the true parameter in the low SNR setting. However, the rate of convergence is significantly slower compared to the high SNR setting. This observation has also been made for vector mixture models
(
Kwon et al. 2021
)
.

Similar as in Theorem
3
, the parameter
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
can be chosen arbitrarily close to 0. Hence, at the expense of increasing the lower bound on the number of iterations by
\log(1/\alpha)
, we can obtain statistically error rates arbitrarily close to
(\sum_{m}d_{m}/n)^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
This slow convergence rate under the low SNR setting was also found for the vector mixture model EM algorithm in
Kwon et al. 2021
.
Compared to
Kwon et al. 2021
, where the convergence rate is a polynomial of the vector dimension
d
, our convergence rate depends on
\sum_{m}d_{m}
instead of
\prod_{m}d_{m}
, dimension of the vectorized tensor. This improvement in rate is again due to the consideration of tensor low-rank structure.

6
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we investigate the finite-sample performance of the proposed
HECM
algorithm and compare it with three existing solutions, including
Kmeans
which applies K-means clustering directly to the vectorized tensor samples, the dynamic tensor clustering method (referred to as
DTC
) proposed by
Sun and Li 2019
and the doubly enhanced EM algorithm (referred to as
DEEM
) proposed by
Mai et al. 2021
.
We focus on the
HECM
algorithm without sample splitting in Algorithm
1
, as it has higher data efficiency in practice.

Let
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\ast}
,
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,m}^{\ast}
,
{\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}
denote respectively the true mean, covariance matrix and precision matrix for all
k,m
.
The evaluation criteria considered include the clustering error (CE) calculated as
\text{CE}=\left|\left\{(i,j):\bm{1}(\hat{z}_{i}=\hat{z}_{j})\neq\bm{1}(z_{i}=z_{j}),i<j\right\}\right|/\binom{n}{2}
, where
\hat{z}_{i}
,
z_{i}
denote the estimated and true cluster labels for
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
, respectively, the cluster mean error (CME) and covariance matrix error (COVME) calculated as

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{CME}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k}\frac{\left\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{k}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\ast}\right\|_{\text{F}}}{\left\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\ast}\right\|_{\text{F}}},\quad\text{COVME}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k}\frac{\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{k,M}\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{\Sigma}_{k,1}-\Sigma_{k,M}^{\ast}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sigma_{k,1}^{\ast}\right\|_{\text{F}}}{\left\|\Sigma_{k,M}^{\ast}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sigma_{k,1}^{\ast}\right\|_{\text{F}}},\end{array}

and the true (TPR) and false positive rates (FPR) in recovering the nonzero entries, i.e.,

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{TPR}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k}\frac{\sum_{m}\sum_{i<j}\bm{1}\left({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}(i,j)\neq 0,\,\,\hat{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}(i,j)\neq 0\right)}{\sum_{m}\sum_{i<j}\bm{1}\left({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}(i,j)\neq 0\right)},\,\text{FPR}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k}\frac{\sum_{m}\sum_{i<j}\bm{1}\left({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}(i,j)=0,\,\,\hat{{\bm{\Omega}}}_{k,m}(i,j)\neq 0\right)}{\sum_{m}\sum_{i<j}\bm{1}\left({\bm{\Omega}}_{k,m}^{\ast}(i,j)=0\right)}.\end{array}

The CE measures the probability of disagreement between the estimated and true cluster labels, and it is commonly considered for evaluating clustering accuracy
(
Sun and Li 2019
)
. The CME and COVME measure the estimation errors for the tensor means and covariance matrices, respectively, while TPR and FPR evaluate the selection accuracy in recovering nonzero entries in the precision matrices.

We consider the third-order case (
M=3
) and generate
n
tensor samples
\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{10\times 10\times 10}
,
i\in[n]
, from the model in
(\ref{eqn:mix})
with four equal-sized clusters. Write
{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast\circ 3}={\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast}\circ{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast}
. We let rank
R=4
and set
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{\ast}
,
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{\ast}
,
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{3}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{4}^{\ast}
as

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{\ast}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast\circ 3},\quad\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{\ast}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}(-1)^{i-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast\circ 3},\quad\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{3}^{\ast}=-\sum_{i=1}^{4}{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast\circ 3},\quad\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{4}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}(-1)^{i}{\bm{\beta}}_{ii}^{\ast\circ 3},

(32)

where
{\bm{\beta}}_{11}^{\ast}=(\mu,\mu,\mu,0,\ldots,0)
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{22}^{\ast}=(0,0,\mu,\mu,\mu,0,\ldots,0)
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{33}^{\ast}=(0,\ldots,0,\mu,\mu,\mu,0,0,0)
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{44}^{\ast}=(0,\ldots,0,\mu,\mu,\mu,0)
.
The parameter
\mu
in the decomposed components controls the signal strength of these four cluster centers. That is, when
\mu
is large, the four clusters are more separated and hence the clustering task is less challenging. Meanwhile,
\mu
also regulates the signal strength in tensor mean estimation.
We set the covariance matrices
{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,m}
,
k\in[K],m\in[M]
, as

{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{k,m}=\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}(\nu)&{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 0}&{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}(\nu)\end{pmatrix},\quad{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}(\nu)=\nu{\bf 1}{\bf 1}^{\top}+(1-\nu){\bf I},

where the parameter
\nu
controls the correlation strength and the level of noise.
